--- Model Response ---
To determine the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes (A and I) in the given graph, let's follow the evaluation process step-by-step.

1. **Examine the two black nodes:**
   - The black nodes are A and I.

2. **Examine the immediate neighboring nodes:**
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of A are E, F, and S.
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of I are H, J, P, and Q.

3. **Iteratively find other neighboring nodes:**
   - A's neighbors:
     - E connects to B and F.
     - F connects to B, J, and T.
     - S connects to F.
   - I's neighbors:
     - H connects to G.
     - J connects to B, F, and Q.
     - P connects to Q.
     - Q connects to F, G, and B.
   - B:
     - Connects to E, N, C, F, and Q.
   - G:
     - Connects to H, Q, and C.

4. **Evaluate the found paths:**
   - Paths from A to I:
     - A -> F -> J -> I
     - A -> F -> B -> Q -> I
     - A -> F -> B -> Q -> G -> H -> I
     - A -> F -> T -> J -> I
     - A -> F -> T -> Q -> I
     - A -> F -> B -> J -> I

5. **Identify the bottleneck:**
   - The edges F and B are critical as they connect multiple paths between A and I.
   - Removing the edges (F, B) and (F, J) will disconnect most paths.

6. **Check if the removal of one edge suffices:**
   - Removing just one edge will not be sufficient as there are multiple paths.
   - Removing (F, B) and (F, J) will disconnect most paths, but we need to ensure all paths are disconnected.

7. **Compare if the removal of edges connected to either one of the black nodes results in a smaller set of edges to be removed:**
   - Removing edges connected to A:
     - (A, F) will disconnect A from several paths, but not all.
   - Removing edges connected to I:
     - (I, J) will disconnect I from several paths, but not all.
   - A combination of removing (F, B) and (F, J) will ensure complete disconnection.

8. **Check your answer:**
   - After removing (F, B) and (F, J), the two black nodes A and I are completely disconnected.

**Output Format:**

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, B), (F, J)].
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 1, 'cut_set': [['H', 'I']]}

1. **Examine the two black nodes:**

   - The immediate neighboring nodes of E are A, B, S, T, and F.
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of J are H, B, and N.

   - From E, we can follow paths to B, S, T, and F.
   - From J, we can follow paths to H, B, and N.
   - We also need to check how these neighboring nodes connect back to each other.

   **From E to J:**
   - E -> B -> J  (this is one path)
   - E -> B -> N  (connects to S,T,F -> B -> J, we follow B as a bottleneck, but check alternatives)
   - E -> B -> S -> T -> B -> J  (huge network of paths can be seen to connect via B)

   - We observe that B is a common node in all possible paths connecting E and J; removing only B may be sufficient, but checking alternatives provides seen all possibilities.

   After removing B, we should check for remaining minimum node-cuts:
      - E -> F -> G -> R -> P ->Q -> I -> N -> J (if anything left to disconnect).

     However, alternative paths still have B as a connection and remaining extra nodes needs to be removed only in case of cross-cut path existence. Hence,

6. **Final Evaluation:**
   - The minimal reducing cut set is sufficient via removing B - evaluating all paths B as a getaway

**Thus, the solution is:**
   Nodes to remove: 1, Cut Set: [B]

1. **Identify the black nodes**: The two black nodes are P and J.
2. **Immediate neighbors of P**: The immediate neighbors of P are Q and I.
3. **Immediate neighbors of J**: The immediate neighbors of J are Q and R.

Next, we trace the paths from P to J:

- From P, through Q to J.
- From P, through Q to R to J.

These are the only direct paths connecting P and J. To disconnect these paths, we can remove any node that is part of these paths.

Given the structure of the graph:
- We must remove either P or J from the path:
  - Removing P ensures no path from P to J exists.
  - Removing Q ensures no path from P to J exists via Q.
  - Removing R ensures no path from P to J exists via Q to R.

Thus, removing any one of these nodes (P, Q, or R) will disconnect P from J.

Since we only need one node removal to disconnect P from J, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 1.

Nodes to remove: 1, Cut Set: [P] or [Q] or [R]

Image name: c+_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: mistral-small-3.1-24b
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 14
--- Model Response ---
To solve this problem, we first need to identify all the paths that connect the two black nodes, B and H.

1. The two black nodes are B and H.
2. The immediate neighboring nodes of B are S, E, F, T, R, J and C.
3. The immediate neighboring nodes of H are I, J, Q, P, G and C.

Let's trace the paths that connect B and H:

- B -> C -> H
- B -> J -> H
- B -> R -> J -> H
- B -> J -> I -> H
- B -> E -> S -> F -> T -> R -> J -> H (though this involves many extra nodes but shows the overall connectivity)
- B -> C -> G -> H

We can see that multiple paths exist between B and H. The key is to find a minimal set of edges to remove that can disconnect these paths.

Upon reviewing these paths, we can see that removing any one of B->C, B->J, and J->H is not sufficient, as another path will remain. However, by analyzing bottlenecks in these paths, we notice that C and J act as intermediate connectors. Thus, we should aim to find a set of edges that removes the connections through these intermediate nodes.

Consider removing the following edges:
- (B, C)
- (H, C)
- (B, J)
- (H, J)

These edges, when removed, totally disconnect B from H.

Since we only need the minimum number of edges to disconnect the two black nodes, we can remove:
- (B, C)
- (H, C)

This is sufficient because it eliminates the direct paths through node C and effectively the remaining paths via node J.

Thus, the answer is:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(B, C), (H, C)].
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'G']]}
